It feels like I'm constantly revved up, as if I've had way too much coffee, so I'm always fidgeting with pens in meetings or bouncing my leg because I can't relax enough to sit still.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping or experiencing restlessness at night due to these constant worries and physical tension?\n\nPatient: Yes, my sleep has been terrible because of this. I lie awake for over an hour replaying client conversations and double-checking numbers in my head, and then I wake up at 3 or 4 in the morning with my mind already racing about next quarter's targets. It feels like I can't ever truly relax my body or brain, even in bed.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks at work or in your personal life because of these worries or fears of not being able to cope with them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've definitely been avoiding my personal and social life lately. I keep turning down weekend trips and canceling on my cycling club and theatre group at the last minute because I feel like I have to work or just stay home and worry. It's gotten to the point where people have actually stopped inviting me to things because I back out so often.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you're avoiding these situations because you fear something specific will happen, like failing or making a mistake, or is it more because the thought of dealing with the situation feels overwhelmingly stressful and you don't know how to cope with the anxiety it triggers?\n\nPatient: It’s not that I’m afraid of failing at cycling or acting; it’s more that my mind is completely consumed by work worries, like missing our sales quotas. The anxiety itself is just so exhausting that I end up canceling to stay home and work, or simply because I feel too on edge to be around other people.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if there was a specific event or period in your life when these excessive worries and avoidance behaviors started, or if they have been a gradual development over time?\n\nPatient: It really started about a year ago, a few months after I was promoted to lead a larger regional sales team. It was a gradual build-up where the constant pressure slowly took over my life, and over the course of the year, I began pulling back from my cycling club and theatre volunteering.\n\nDoctor: Did the excessive worry and avoidance behaviors start immediately after the promotion, or was there a specific incident or realization after the promotion that seemed to trigger the escalation of these symptoms?\n\nPatient: No, it didn't start immediately on day one of the promotion. It built up gradually over a few months as the reality of the longer hours, constant performance scrutiny, and responsibility for my team's numbers really set in. There wasn't one specific incident, but after a few months, the worry just became a steady, hard-to-shut-off presence that I couldn't escape.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic event in the past that might be contributing to your current state of anxiety and worry, or is this primarily related to the stress of your job promotion?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't experienced any past traumatic events; this is entirely related to my job promotion from about 14 months ago.
